Detection and Mode-Identification of Multiple Change Points in Tensor Factor Models
Yuqi Zhang, Zetai Cen, Haeran Cho

Abstract
We study the problems arising from modeling high-dimensional tensor-valued time series under a Tucker decomposition-based factor model with multiple structural change points. First, we propose an algorithm for detecting the multiple change points, which utilizes the low-rank structure of the data for statistical and computational efficiency. Also, the multi-dimensional array setting poses unique challenges, as some changes are associated with a subset of the modes, and the changes in different modes may interact with one another. Recognizing these, we investigate the problem of identifying each change with the tensor modes post-segmentation. To this end, we formalize the mode-identifiability of each change and propose an algorithm for detecting the modes at which the data are undergoing a mode-identifiable shift. We establish the consistency of both change point detection and mode-identification methods under a weak moment condition, and demonstrate their good performance on simulated datasets where, in particular, it is shown that the mode-identification step can improve the post-segmentation estimation of the mode-wise loading space. Additionally we analyze the datasets on New York City taxi usage and Fama–French portfolio returns using the proposed suite of methods.



Key words and phrases:

Change point detection,
mode-identifiability,
tensor factor model,
Tucker decomposition.

1
Introduction

High-dimensional time series are increasingly observed in the form of tensors, where each observation is a multi-dimensional array organized along several modes. Examples include dynamic transport and trade networks across product groups, multi-dimensional finance panels indexed by firms, characteristics and time, spatiotemporal environmental fields, and biomedical imaging data, to name a few
(
43
;
19
;
51
;
2
)
.
Such tensor formatting not only preserves the innate interpretation of the data, but also can underline inherent structure therein.

Tensor factor models provide a natural framework for analyzing such data by imposing a low-rank representation.
Specifically, each tensor-valued observation is assumed to be driven by a handful of latent factors, as characterized by some mode-specific loading matrices, thereby achieving dimension reduction while preserving the role of each mode
(
54
;
19
;
32
)
.
Based on the two popular tensor decompositions
(
40
)
, Tucker and CanDecomp/PARAFAC (CP) factor models have frequently been studied, where the latter is a special case of the former, and the recent literature has seen a wide range of developments from determining the ranks
(
31
)
, to estimating the mode-wise loading spaces and latent factors
(
20
;
30
;
12
;
10
)
and handling missing observations by imputation
(
16
)
.

While most of these contributions are made assuming that the factor structure is time-invariant, departure from such an assumption is often observed in many applications.
Macroeconomic and financial dependence patterns may shift after major policy changes, crises or market re-organizations, leading to structural changes in how certain factors drive the observed time series
(
53
;
14
;
18
)
. In these scenarios, ignoring changes could undermine parameter estimation and mask the interpretation of how the latent structure evolves.

The problem of change point detection has studied extensively in vector time series factor modeling.
Existing work includes tests for structural instability in factor loadings
(
18
;
58
;
6
)
, estimation of a single break date
(
6
;
25
;
26
)
, inference for break dates
(
3
)
, and multiple change point detection algorithms
(
17
;
9
;
7
;
44
;
3
;
11
)
. By contrast, the problem of detecting change points in matrix or, more generally, tensor time series remains largely unexplored. For matrix factor models with potential changes,
33

proposed an online monitoring scheme, while
48
;
49
developed offline procedures.
Beyond the matrix settings,
35
;
47
study the problem of detecting changes in the mean of a tensor-variate sequence,
62
;
55
investigate the problem in dynamic networks, and
1
propose a practical approach to detect changes in cryptocurrency networks by leveraging CP decomposition.

Despite the growing interests in tensor data with change points, a formal treatment towards offline detection in tensor factor models is still absent, to the best of our knowledge.
Moreover, due to the multi-dimensional array nature of the data, changes in a tensor factor model may affect one or several modes of the tensor data and manifest differently across modes, and these changes in different modes may interact with one another.
Vectorizing the data would annihilate this property, thus obscuring the relations between any detected change and the tensor modes, which calls for methods that preserve and exploit the tensor structure.

Recognizing these new challenges, we develop a suite of methods for modeling tensor time series under a factor model with multiple changes.
Firstly, we propose an algorithm for detecting multiple change points in the tensor factor model.
It extends the idea often utilized in the vector factor modeling literature, that different types of changes in the factor structure are observationally equivalent to the changes in the covariance of so-called pseudo-factor of a fixed dimension
(
29
;
7
;
25
)
, to the tensor setting.
This is particularly beneficial for the tensor-valued data that quickly becomes high-dimensional: For a series of order-
K
tensors
\mathcal{X}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\dots\times p_{K}}
, our proposed method performs multiple change point detection on the objects of dimension
\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}(r_{k}+1)/2
, where
r_{k}
’s denote the dimensions of the order-
K
pseudo-factor tensor which are typically much smaller than
p_{k}
.

We also formulate and study the problem of mode-identification for tensor factor models with multiple change points, a first contribution of its kind.
We formally characterize the
mode-identifiability
of a change, which enables the selection of the tensor modes that undergo identifiable shifts at each change point once the change points are detected.
A direct downstream benefit of mode-identification is that it enables
mode-informed
loading space estimation which brings demonstrable numerical benefits over estimating the loading spaces segment by segment.
Under a weak moment condition, we establish the consistency of the combined methodology, both in estimating the total number and locations of the change points and in selecting the subset of modes undergoing identifiable changes at each change point.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces the tensor factor model with multiple change points and defines the notion of mode-identifiability of a change.
In Section
3
, we describe the methods for multiple change point detection and post-detection mode-identification, with a detailed discussion on the selection of tuning parameters.
Section
4
presents the assumptions and establishes the asymptotic consistency of the proposed methods.
Section
5
reports the simulation results and in Section
6
, we analyze the datasets on New York City taxi usage and Fama–French portfolio returns.
Section
7
concludes the paper, and all the proofs, additional discussions and simulation results are deferred to the supplement.

Notations.

Given a positive integer
m
, we write
[m]=\{1,2,\dots,m\}
.
For sequences
\{a_{n}\}
and
\{b_{n}\}
, We use
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
to denote
a_{n}=\mathcal{O}(b_{n})
,
a_{n}\gtrsim b_{n}
to denote
b_{n}=\mathcal{O}(a_{n})
, and
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
to denote
a_{n}=\mathcal{O}(b_{n})
and
b_{n}=\mathcal{O}(a_{n})
; the stochastic boundness is denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{P}(\cdot)
.
For a vector
u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{p})^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, let
|u|_{2}=(\sum_{i=1}^{p}u_{i}^{2})^{1/2}
denote the Euclidean norm.
For a matrix
A=[a_{ij}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, let
\|A\|
denote the operator norm,
\|A\|_{F}=(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{ij}^{2})^{1/2}
the Frobenius norm,
\|A\|_{1}=\max_{1\leq j\leq n}\sum_{i=1}^{m}|a_{ij}|
and
\|A\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|a_{ij}|
denote the induced
\ell_{1}
- and
\ell_{\infty}
-norm, respectively. For a matrix
A
, let
\mathrm{col}(A)
denote its column space.
Also,
\mathrm{vec}(A)
denotes the vector in
\mathbb{R}^{mn}
obtained by stacking the columns of
A
in column-major order and for a symmetric matrix
A
, let
\mathrm{vech}(A)
denote the vector obtained by stacking the lower triangular entries of
A
(including the diagonal) in column-major order.
For an order-
K
tensor
\mathcal{X}=(X_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{K}})\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\cdots\times p_{K}}
, with
p=\prod_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}
and
p_{\text{-}k}=p/p_{k}
,
we denote by
\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{k}\times p_{\text{-}k}}
its
mode-
k
unfolding/matricization
, and by
\mathcal{X}\times_{k}A
the
mode-
k
product
of a tensor
\mathcal{X}
with a matrix
A
, defined by
\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{X}\times_{k}A)=A\,\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{X})
.
Finally, we use
\otimes
to represent the Kronecker product, and
\circ
the Hadamard product.

2
Model

2.1
Tensor factor model with multiple change points

Consider a mean-zero time series of order-
K
tensors
\mathcal{X}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\dots\times p_{K}},\,t\in[T]
, with
K\geq 1
, which follows a Tucker tensor factor model with
q
change points such that

\mathcal{X}_{t}=\mathcal{C}_{t}+\mathcal{E}_{t},\text{ \ where \ }\mathcal{C}_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{F}_{t}\times_{k=1}^{K}\Lambda_{1,k}&\text{for $\theta_{0}+1=1\leq t\leq\theta_{1}$,}\\
\mathcal{F}_{t}\times_{k=1}^{K}\Lambda_{2,k}&\text{for $\theta_{1}+1\leq t\leq\theta_{2}$,}\\
\vdots\\
\mathcal{F}_{t}\times_{k=1}^{K}\Lambda_{q+1,k}&\text{for $\theta_{q}+1\leq t\leq\theta_{q+1}=T$,}\end{array}\right.

(2.1)

where
\theta_{0}=0
,
\theta_{q+1}=T
and
\Theta=\{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{q}\}\subseteq[T]
denotes the set of change point locations.
Here,
\mathcal{F}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times\dots\times r_{K}}
denotes the core factor tensor with its dimensions
r_{k}
’s fixed regardless of growing
p_{k}
,
\mathcal{C}_{t}
denotes the common component driven by the core factor, and
\mathcal{E}_{t}
is the idiosyncratic component unaccounted by
\mathcal{F}_{t}
.
We denote by
\Lambda_{j,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{k}\times r_{k}}
the mode-
k
factor loading matrix in the
j
-th segment such that at each change point
\theta_{j}
, at least one of the loading matrices undergoes a shift.
We permit the numbers of factors to change such that the column rank of each
\Lambda_{j,k}
, which effectively represents the mode-
k
factor number in the
j
-th segment, may be strictly smaller than
r_{k}
; later we introduce an alternative representation of the model (
2.1
) with which the meaning of
r_{k}
is made clearer.
Throughout, we make the following condition on
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
:

Condition 1
.

At any
t\in[T]
, the elements of the core factor
\mathcal{F}_{t}
are uncorrelated with each other and have zero mean and unit variance, i.e.
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}(\mathrm{vec}(\mathcal{F}_{t}))=I_{r}
.

For each given
j
, while the space spanned by the columns of each
\Lambda_{j,k}
, i.e.
\mathrm{col}(\Lambda_{j,k})
, is uniquely defined, the loading matrices and the core factor tensor are not identifiable regardless of
K
.
From this perspective, Condition
1
is akin to the assumption commonly found in the literature on vector factor modeling
(
5
)
, if
\mathcal{F}_{t}
admits a separable covariance structure
(
34
)
, i.e.
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}(\mathrm{vec}(\mathcal{F}_{t}))=\otimes_{k=K}^{1}\Gamma^{(k)}_{F}
for some positive definite
\Gamma^{(k)}_{F}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{k}\times r_{k}}
, whereby the rotation and normalization therein can be absorbed into the factor loadings.
Later we make Assumption
1
on the core factor time series which, while fulfilling Condition
1
, further characterizes the degree of serial dependence.
Even under Condition
1
, we have
\Lambda_{j,k}
identifiable up to an orthogonal transform, see Lemma
B.2
in the supplement.
Moreover, changes in the loading matrices may equivalently be represented as the changes in the covariance of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
and vice versa:

Example 1
.

Suppose that we observe
\mathcal{X}_{t}=\mathcal{G}_{t}\times_{k=1}^{K}\Lambda_{k}+\mathcal{E}_{t},\,t\in[T]
, where
\{\mathcal{G}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
undergoes a single change point in its covariance, as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{F}_{t}\\
\mathcal{F}_{t}\times_{k=1}^{K}A_{k}\\
\end{array}\right.\text{ \ whereby \ }\mathbb{C}\mathrm{ov}(\mathrm{vec}(\mathcal{G}_{t}))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}I_{r}=I_{r_{K}}\otimes\cdots\otimes I_{r_{1}}&\text{for \ }1\leq t\leq\theta_{1},\\
\Gamma_{G}^{(K)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Gamma_{G}^{(1)}&\text{for \ }\theta_{1}+1\leq t\leq T,\end{array}\right.

with
\mathcal{F}_{t}
satisfying Condition
1
, and
\Gamma_{G}^{(k)}=A_{k}A_{k}^{\intercal}\neq I_{r_{k}}
for at least one
k\in[K]
.
Then, we may re-write this time series as in (
2.1
), with
\mathcal{F}_{t}
as the core factor and segment-wise mode-
k
loading matrices
\Lambda_{1,k}=\Lambda_{k}
and
\Lambda_{2,k}=\Lambda_{k}A_{k}
.

Example
1
demonstrates that the model in (
2.1
) accommodates different types of changes arising from the Tucker tensor factor model; see also Section
A.1
for further example scenarios.

In fact, there exists a global mode-
k
loading matrix
\Lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{k}\times r_{k}}
such that for each
\Lambda_{j,k}
, there is a transformation matrix
A_{j,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{k}\times r_{k}}
, not necessarily of full rank, with which we can write
\Lambda_{j,k}=\Lambda_{k}A_{j,k}
; we set
r_{k}
to be the smallest integer so that such
\Lambda_{k}
is of full column rank.
Then, the model in (
2.1
) is equivalently re-written as

\begin{split}\mathcal{X}_{t}&=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{q+1}\mathcal{F}_{t}\times_{k=1}^{K}A_{j,k}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\{\theta_{j-1}<t\leq\theta_{j}\}}\right)\times_{k=1}^{K}\Lambda_{k}+\mathcal{E}_{t}=:\mathcal{G}_{t}\times_{k=1}^{K}\Lambda_{k}+\mathcal{E}_{t},\end{split}

(2.2)

where
\mathcal{G}_{t}
is the pseudo-factor tensor of the same dimensions as
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, and is piecewise stationary with the change points in its covariance at
\theta_{j},\,j\in[q]
.
To see this, at any
\theta_{j}
, note that there exists some
k\in[K]
such that the corresponding mode-
k
covariance of
\mathcal{G}_{t}
undergoes a change, as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\{\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{G}_{\theta_{j}})\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{G}_{\theta_{j}})^{\intercal}\Big\}=A_{j,k}\mathbb{E}\Big\{\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{F}_{\theta_{j}})A_{j,\text{-}k}^{\intercal}A_{j,\text{-}k}\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{F}_{\theta_{j}})^{\intercal}\Big\}A_{j,k}^{\intercal}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\textnormal{\text{tr}}\Big(A_{j,\text{-}k}^{\intercal}A_{j,\text{-}k}\Big)\cdot A_{j,k}A_{j,k}^{\intercal}\neq\textnormal{\text{tr}}\Big(A_{j+1,\text{-}k}^{\intercal}A_{j+1,\text{-}k}\Big)\cdot A_{j+1,k}A_{j+1,k}^{\intercal}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}\Big\{\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{G}_{\theta_{j}+1})\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{G}_{\theta_{j}+1})^{\intercal}\Big\},

(2.3)

with
A_{j,\text{-}k}:=\otimes_{i=K,i\neq k}^{1}A_{j,i}
, where the second and last equalities hold under Condition
1
(see Lemma
B.3
in the supplement). The re-formulation in (
2.2
) extends the idea popularly exploited for change point detection under vector factor models to higher-order tensor time series, that structural changes in the factor loading can equivalently be represented as changes in the (mode-wise) covariance matrix of the pseudo-factor.
Thus motivated, we define the size of change at
\theta_{j}
.

Definition 1

(Size of change)
.

Let

\Gamma_{G,(j)}^{(k)}=\mathbb{E}\{\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{G}_{t})\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{G}_{t})^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\{\theta_{j-1}+1\leq t\leq\theta_{j}\}}\}
for each
j\in[q+1]
and
k\in[K]
.
Then the size of change in mode-
k
at
\theta_{j}
is defined as
\omega_{j}^{(k)}=\|\Omega_{j}^{(k)}\|_{F}
with
\Omega_{j}^{(k)}=\Gamma_{G,(j+1)}^{(k)}-\Gamma_{G,(j)}^{(k)}
.
With these, we define the size of change at
\theta_{j}
as
\omega_{j}=\big\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\big(\omega_{j}^{(k)}\big)^{2}\big\}^{1/2}
.

2.2
Mode-identifiability at each change point

Inspecting (
2.3
) reveals that the size of change
\omega^{(k)}_{j}
in Definition
1
, depends not only on the change in the mode-
k
transformation matrix, but also those corresponding to other modes.
Accordingly, the tensor setting with
K>1
brings complications previously unobserved in the vector factor modeling literature, and naturally raises the following questions.

(Q1)

Can we identify all the modes that undergo a shift at the
j
-th change point, i.e. all
k\in[K]
such that
A_{j,k}\neq A_{j+1,k}
?

(Q2)

If multiple modes have
A_{j,k}\neq A_{j+1,k}
at the
j
-th change point, can these changes cancel each other out, leading to
\omega_{j}=0
?

Although the problem of change point detection under matrix factor models has previously been studied, these questions have not been addressed:

33
focus on the situation where a change occurs in a single mode only, in developing and analyzing an online change test;
48
;
49
propose to detect changes in the covariance of
\mathrm{vec}(\mathcal{G}_{t})
and thus ignore the cross-mode interactions.
We make a first contribution in addressing the inherently unique challenges brought by the tensor setting.
To this end, we first define the notion of mode-identifiability of a change.

Definition 2

(Mode-identification of changes)
.

Consider the model in (
2.2
) and suppose that Condition
1
holds.
Then at
\theta_{j}
for some
j\in[q]
, the mode-
k
change from
A_{j,k}
to
A_{j+1,k}
is defined to be mode-unidentifiable if and only if there exists a set of matrices
\{B_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[K]\setminus\{k\}}
such that

\mathcal{F}_{t}\times_{\ell\in[K]\setminus\{k\}}A_{j+1,\ell}\times_{k}A_{j+1,k}=\mathcal{F}_{t}\times_{\ell\in[K]\setminus\{k\}}B_{\ell}\times_{k}A_{j,k}.

Otherwise the change point
\theta_{j}
is mode-
k
identifiable. A change point is referred to as mode-unidentifiable when it is mode-
k
unidentifiable for all
k\in[K]
; otherwise it is mode-identifiable.

With Definition
2
, we can associate each change point
\theta_{j}
with the subset of tensor modes
k\in[K]
for which it is mode-identifiable,
such that characterizing the mode-
k
change only depends upon the relation between
A_{j,k}
and
A_{j+1,k}
regardless of the transformation matrices at modes
\ell\neq k
.
We justify this notion of mode-(un)identifiability with the following theorem.

Theorem 1

(The only type of mode-unidentifiable change)
.

Consider the model in (
2.2
) and suppose that Condition
1
holds.
Then for any
j\in[q]
and
k\in[K]
, the mode-
k
change is mode-unidentifiable for
\theta_{j}
if and only if
A_{j+1,k}=cA_{j,k}Q
for some constant
c
and orthogonal matrix
Q
.

Notice that under Condition
1
, the transformation matrices
A_{j}
are identifiable up to orthogonal transforms (cf. Lemma
B.2
).
Therefore, Theorem
1
establishes that the only type of mode-unidentifiable change is in the form of a scalar multiplication.
Jointly, Definition
2
and Theorem
1
allow us to regard the changes across different modes individually in higher-order tensor factor models.
Back to the earlier questions, Theorem
1
directly addresses
(Q1)
.
As for
(Q2)
, combined with Lemma
B.1
in the supplement, the theorem shows that any mode-identifiable change is detectable (in the sense that
\omega_{j}>0
) and, in fact, the cross-mode cancellation of the change occurs in the trivial scenario only: When
\theta_{j}
is mode-unidentifiable with the scalar multiples on all modes canceling one another.
For completeness, we further categorize the types of mode-identifiable changes in Section
A.2
.

In summary, the model (
2.1
) poses an interesting challenge of mode-identification which, following the detection of multiple change points, can enrich their interpretation and reveal cross-mode interactions.
We propose a mode-identification algorithm in Section
3.2
that accounts for the stochasticity arising from the change point detection step.

3
Methodology

3.1
Change point detection

Recall that under the model (
2.2
), we are able to detect the change points by scanning for the changes in the covariance of the global pseudo-factor tensor
\mathcal{G}_{t}
.
As a first step for estimating the unobservable
\mathcal{G}_{t}
, we estimate the mode-
k
loading matrix
\Lambda_{k}
from the (scaled) mode-
k
sample covariance matrix of
\mathcal{X}_{t}
for each
k\in[K]
, i.e.
\widehat{\Gamma}_{X}^{(k)}=(Tp)^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{X}_{t})\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{X}_{t})^{\intercal}
.
In the time series tensor factor modeling literature, a popularly adopted estimation approach takes two steps:
We first perform eigendecomposition on
\widehat{\Gamma}_{X}^{(k)}
, and retain the
r_{k}
leading eigenvectors multiplied by
\sqrt{p_{k}}
, as the preliminary estimator
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}
.
Then, writing

\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\text{-}k}=\otimes_{j\in[K]\setminus\{k\}}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k},\quad Y_{k,t}=\frac{1}{p_{\text{-}k}}\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{X}_{t})\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\text{-}k},\text{\ and \ }\widehat{\Gamma}_{Y}^{(k)}=\frac{1}{Tp_{k}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Y_{k,t}Y_{k,t}^{\intercal},

(3.1)

we obtain a refined estimator
\widehat{\Lambda}_{k}
as
\sqrt{p_{k}}
times the
r_{k}
leading eigenvectors of
\widehat{\Gamma}_{Y}^{(k)}
.
The additional step of projecting
\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{X}_{t})
onto the column space of
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\text{-}k}
, leads to improved estimation performance both theoretically and numerically
(
59
;
61
;
12
)
.
This approach is justifiable under the orthogonality imposed on
\Lambda_{k}
, see Assumption
2
below.
Then, the pseudo-factor tensor is estimated as
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{t}=p^{-1}\mathcal{X}_{t}\times_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\Lambda}_{k}^{\intercal},\,t\in[T]
.

For effectively scanning for the changes in the mode-wise covariance of
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{t}
, we construct a cumulative sum (CUSUM) statistic.
For its definition and motivation, let us write

\widehat{\Gamma}_{G,a,b}^{(k)}=\frac{1}{b-a}\sum_{t=a+1}^{b}\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{t})\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{t})^{\intercal}\text{ \ and \ }{\Gamma}_{G,a,b}^{(k)}=\frac{1}{b-a}\sum_{t=a+1}^{b}\mathbb{E}\left\{\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{G}_{t})\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{G}_{t})^{\intercal}\right\},

(3.2)

for any
k\in[K]
and
0\leq a<b\leq T
.
We have, for each
j\in[q]
and any integers
a,b
such that
\theta_{j-1}\leq a<\theta_{j}<b\leq\theta_{j+1}
,

\displaystyle{\Gamma}_{G,\tau,b}^{(k)}-{\Gamma}_{G,a,\tau}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{b-\theta_{j}}{b-\tau}\Big({\Gamma}_{G,(j+1)}^{(k)}-{\Gamma}_{G,(j)}^{(k)}\Big)&\text{for $a<\tau\leq\theta_{j}$,}\\
\frac{\theta_{j}-a}{\tau-a}\Big({\Gamma}_{G,(j+1)}^{(k)}-{\Gamma}_{G,(j)}^{(k)}\Big)&\text{for $\theta_{j}+1\leq\tau\leq b$.}\end{array}\right.

Then, provided that
\omega_{j}^{(k)}\neq 0
(see Definition
1
), any matrix norm of
{\Gamma}_{G,\tau,b}^{(k)}-{\Gamma}_{G,a,\tau}^{(k)}
is maximized at
\tau=\theta_{j}
, which justifies the scanning of its estimated counterpart for change point detection.
Instead of detecting the change points separately for each mode, we advocate an approach that searches for the changes in all
K
modes simultaneously.
The latter enhances the detection power by pooling the information across multiple modes, while avoiding any necessity for post-processing as a change in mode
k
at
\theta_{j}
may manifest itself in different modes with
\omega^{(k^{\prime})}_{j}\neq 0
for some
k^{\prime}\neq k
, see (
2.3
) and the discussions in Section
2.2
.
To this end, we consider the following CUSUM statistics over appropriately selected intervals
(a,b],\,0\leq a<b\leq T
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{a,\tau,b}&=\sqrt{\frac{(\tau-a)(b-\tau)}{b-a}}\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{vech}\left(\widehat{\Gamma}_{G,\tau,b}^{(1)}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{G,a,\tau}^{(1)}\right)\\
\vdots\\
\mathrm{vech}\left(\widehat{\Gamma}_{G,\tau,b}^{(K)}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{G,a,\tau}^{(K)}\right)\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\text{ \ for \ }a<\tau<b,\end{split}

with
d=\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}(r_{k}+1)/2
. Then finally, with a (possibly) location-dependent, positive definite weight matrix
W_{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we define the detector statistic

\mathcal{T}_{a,\tau,b}=\big|\mathcal{M}_{a,\tau,b}^{\intercal}W_{\tau}^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{a,\tau,b}\big|^{1/2}.

(3.3)

We propose to scan the data for multiple changes using
\mathcal{T}_{a,\tau,b}
, looking for local maximizers of the detector statistic over strategically selected intervals
(a,b]
.
Specifically, we embed this statistic in a multiscale search based on seeded intervals
(
41
)
and the narrowest-over-threshold principle
(
8
)
, the combination of which has successfully been applied to a wide range of change point detection problems.

Specifically, we evaluate
\mathcal{T}_{a,\tau,b}
over a collection of the seeded intervals generated as

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}=\bigcup_{h=1}^{\lfloor\mu_{T}\rfloor}\left\{\Big(\big\lfloor(i-1)m_{h}\big\rfloor,\big\lceil(i+1)m_{h}\big\rceil\Big]:\;i\in\big[\lceil T/m_{h}\rceil-1\big],\;m_{h}=T/2^{h}\right\},

(3.4)

with
\mu_{T}\asymp\log_{2}\log(T)
.
By construction, the number of intervals in
\mathbb{M}
is
\mathcal{O}(\log(T))
, and the lengths of the intervals are at least of order
T/\log(T)
.
In the literature on change point detection under factor models, it is often assumed that the change points are linearly spaced (see Assumption
3
), which ensures that pseudo-factors induced by the changes are well-captured by the global estimation procedure leading to
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{t},\,t\in[T]
.
This allows us, with the thus-constructed
\mathbb{M}
, to systematically zoom in the neighborhoods of individual change points and isolate each in a sufficiently large seeded interval for its detection and estimation.

Next, we iteratively select the shortest interval, say
(a,b]\in\mathbb{M}
, over which the maximal local covariance difference aggregated over the
K
modes, as measured by
\max_{a+\varpi_{T}<\tau<b-\varpi_{T}}\mathcal{T}_{a,\tau,b}
, exceeds a given threshold
\pi_{T,p}
; if there are ties, we select the interval with the largest detector statistic.
The trimming parameter
\varpi_{T}
, as well as the choice of the finest level
\mu_{T}
for the seeded interval construction, are introduced to effectively control for the errors arising from scanning the data multiple times, which becomes more challenging under the weaker moment condition we impose on the data (see Assumptions
1
and
4
below).
This step returns a seeded interval containing exactly one change point with high probability, effectively breaking down the multiple change point problem to that of detecting each change point individually.
Then we identify a change point estimator as
\widehat{\theta}=\mathop{\arg\max}_{a+\varpi_{T}<\tau<b-\varpi_{T}}\mathcal{T}_{a,\tau,b}
, remove any intervals in
\mathbb{M}
containing
\widehat{\theta}
from future consideration, and repeatedly perform the above steps until no seeded interval is left to be considered.
Referred to as
t
ensor
f
actor
m
odel
seg
mentation algorithm (TFMseg), its full description is given in Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1

TFMseg: Multiple change point detection in tensor factor models

1:

Input:
Trimming parameter
\varpi_{T}\geq 0
, threshold
\pi_{T,p}\geq 0
, seeded interval collection
\mathbb{M}\leftarrow\{(a_{\ell},b_{\ell}],\,\ell\in[M]\}
from (
3.4
)

2:

Initialize:
Set
\widehat{\Theta}\leftarrow\emptyset
and
\mathbb{L}\leftarrow[M]

3:

for

\ell\in\mathbb{L}

do

4:

if

b_{\ell}-a_{\ell}>2\varpi_{T}

then

5:

Set
\tau_{\ell}\leftarrow\argmax_{a_{\ell}+\varpi_{T}<\tau<b_{\ell}-\varpi_{T}}\mathcal{T}_{a_{\ell},\tau,b_{\ell}}

and
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\leftarrow\mathcal{T}_{a_{\ell},\tau_{\ell},b_{\ell}}

6:

else

7:

Set
\tau_{\ell}\leftarrow b_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\leftarrow 0

8:

if

\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\leq\pi_{T,p}

then

9:

Set
\mathbb{L}\leftarrow\mathbb{L}\setminus\{\ell\}

10:

while

\mathbb{L}\neq\emptyset

do

11:

Set
\ell^{\circ}\leftarrow\argmin_{\ell\in\mathbb{L}}(b_{\ell}-a_{\ell})
; if there are ties, select the index with the largest
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}

12:

Set
\widehat{\theta}\leftarrow\tau_{\ell^{\circ}}
,
\widehat{\Theta}\leftarrow\widehat{\Theta}\cup\{\widehat{\theta}\}
and
\mathbb{L}\leftarrow\mathbb{L}\setminus\{\ell\in\mathbb{L}:\widehat{\theta}\in(a_{\ell},b_{\ell}]\}

13:

Output:
Set of change point estimators
\widehat{\Theta}

Remark 1

(Utilizing mode-wise separability for change point detection.)
.

In the context of matrix factor time series segmentation,
48
;
49
propose to search for change points in the
r\times r
covariance of
\vec{(}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{t})
where
r=\prod_{k=1}^{3}r_{k}
.
While not reported here, in preliminary numerical experiments, we considered its tensor-analogue by modifying the CUSUM statistic
\mathcal{M}_{a,\tau,b}
accordingly while keeping the rest of the algorithm identical, and found that the resultant procedure suffered from the relatively large dimensionality.
For example, for the order-
3
tensor factor time series in Section
5.1
with
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})=(3,3,3)
, this approach handles a CUSUM process of dimension
27\times 28/2=378
, compared to
3\times 3\times 4/2=18
in the case of TFMseg.
This illustrates the advantage of the proposed detector statistic which utilizes the separability of the covariance of
\mathcal{G}_{t}
implied by Condition
1
, in further reducing the dimensionality of the structure of interest from
r(r+1)/2
to
\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}(r_{k}+1)/2
, the gap between which grows with the tensor order
K
.

Remark 2

(Change point detection with missing data)
.

Often the data contain missingness particularly in high-dimensional time series. Analyzing a fully-observed subset of the data only, may significantly reduce the realized sample size and potentially compromise the interpretation of the outcome, all of which may be intensified for tensor data.
Common techniques to circumvent missing observations can be categorized as likelihood-based methods
(
45
;
28
)
, inverse probability weighting
(
36
;
50
)
, and imputation
(
37
;
24
)
, and the problem of handling missingness in the change point detection framework has attracted some attention
(
57
;
27
;
46
)
.
We may leverage an imputation procedure proposed by
16
for tensor time series admitting a representation in (
2.1
) (without change points).
Specifically, noting that the detector statistic in (
3.3
) only requires some estimator of the core factors, we may employ the estimated factor series (which contains no missing data) in Equation (3.7) of
16
to plug in (
3.2
), with the rest of the steps of TFMseg remaining the same.
We empirically validate this modification of TFMseg in Section
6.2
and Appendix
D.5
.

3.2
Post-detection mode-identification

Once the set of change point estimators
\widehat{\Theta}=\{\widehat{\theta}_{j},\,j\in[\widehat{q}]:\,\widehat{\theta}_{1}<\ldots<\widehat{\theta}_{\widehat{q}}\}
is returned by Algorithm
1
, we identify the subset of modes for which each
\widehat{\theta}_{j}
is identifiable according to Definition
2
.
For this, we measure the degree of the mode-
k
identifiability at change point
\theta_{j}
with
\|\Xi_{j}^{(k)}\|
where, with
\Gamma_{G,(j+1)}^{(k)}
given in Definition
1
,

\Xi_{j}^{(k)}:=\textnormal{\text{tr}}\Big(\Gamma_{G,(j+1)}^{(k)}\Big)^{-1}\Gamma_{G,(j+1)}^{(k)}-\textnormal{\text{tr}}\Big(\Gamma_{G,(j)}^{(k)}\Big)^{-1}\Gamma_{G,(j)}^{(k)}.

(3.5)

By Theorem
1
and (
2.3
), if the change at
\theta_{j}
is mode-unidentifiable, we have
\|\Xi_{j}^{(k)}\|=0
. More importantly, together with Lemma
B.1
in the supplement,
\|\Xi_{j}^{(k)}\|=0
only if the change is mode-unidentifiable.
In the case of
c=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\|A_{j,k}\|_{F}=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\|A_{j+1,k}\|_{F}
, we have
\omega_{j}^{(k)}
directly related to
\|\Xi_{j}^{(k)}\|
as
\omega_{j}^{(k)}=c\cdot\|\Xi_{j}^{(k)}\|
.
That is, if the overall magnitude of the transformation matrices remains invariant, the difficulty in detecting the change using its mode information is proportional to the difficulty in mode-identification.
Then, our aim is to identify
{\mathcal{K}}_{j}:=\{k\in[K]:\,\|\Xi^{(k)}_{j}\|>0\}
for all
j\in[q]
, with
{\mathcal{K}}_{j}=\emptyset
if and only if the change at
\theta_{j}
is mode-unidentifiable.

For the estimation of
\Xi_{j}^{(k)}
, we propose to adopt the set of
finer
seeded intervals,

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}^{*}=\left\{\Big(\big\lfloor(i-1)m^{*}\big\rfloor,\big\lceil(i+1)m^{*}\big\rceil\Big]:\;i\in\big[\lceil T/m^{*}\rceil-1\big],\;m^{*}=T/2^{\mu_{T}+1}\right\},

with
\mu_{T}
used in the construction of
\mathbb{M}
in (
3.4
).
Compared to
\mathbb{M}
, the set
\mathbb{M}^{*}
contains single-scale seeded intervals at the finer scale
\mu_{T}+1
.
Setting
\widehat{\theta}_{0}=\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{+}=0
and
\widehat{\theta}_{\widehat{q}+1}=\widehat{\theta}_{\widehat{q}+1}^{-}=T
, we define

\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{-}:=\max\left\{a:(a,b]\in\mathbb{M}^{*},b\leq\widehat{\theta}_{j}\right\}\text{ \ and \ }\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{+}:=\min\left\{b:(a,b]\in\mathbb{M}^{*},a>\widehat{\theta}_{j}\right\},

(3.6)

for each
j\in[\widehat{q}]
.
With these,
\Gamma^{(k)}_{G,(j)},\,k\in[K]
, are estimated as
\widehat{\Gamma}_{G,(j)}^{(k)}=\widehat{\Gamma}_{G,\widehat{\theta}_{j-1}^{+},\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{-}}^{(k)}
, see (
3.2
).
In doing so, upon the consistent estimation of
\Theta
by
\widehat{\Theta}
,
we select
(\widehat{\theta}_{j-1}^{+},\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{-}]
from the end points of the intervals
\mathbb{M}^{*}
to be the maximal interval lying between
\theta_{j-1}
and
\theta_{j}
with probability tending to one.
At the same time, selecting
\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{\pm}
from the end points of the deterministic seeded intervals in
\mathbb{M}^{*}
, allows us to control for multiplicity arising from the stochasticity of
\widehat{\Theta}
effectively.
Next, we estimate
\Xi_{j}^{(k)}
by
\widehat{\Xi}_{j}^{(k)}
obtained according to (
3.5
) by plugging in
\widehat{\Gamma}_{G,(j)}^{(k)}
in place of
{\Gamma}_{G,(j)}^{(k)}
.
Finally, for each
j\in[\widehat{q}]
, we determine the change detected by
\widehat{\theta}_{j}
to be mode-
k
identifiable if
\|\widehat{\Xi}_{j}^{(k)}\|>\zeta_{T,p}
for some threshold
\zeta_{T,p}
, i.e.
\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}_{j}=\{k\in[K]:\,\|\widehat{\Xi}_{j}^{(k)}\|>\zeta_{T,p}\}
.
We conclude that change detected by
\widehat{\theta}_{j}
as mode-unidentifiable if
\|\widehat{\Xi}_{j}^{(k)}\|\leq\zeta_{T,p}
for all
k\in[K]
or, equivalently, if
\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}_{j}=\emptyset
.

Remark 3

(Mode-informed estimation of loading spaces)
.

Once the change points are detected as in Section
3.1
, we may estimate the mode-
k
loading space for the
j
-th segment,
\textnormal{span}(\Lambda_{j,k})
, using the section of the data over the interval
(\widehat{\theta}_{j-1},\widehat{\theta}_{j}]
. However,
\textnormal{span}(\Lambda_{j,k})
for some mode-
k
does not necessarily vary at every change point, either as
A_{j,k}=A_{j+1,k}
, or as the change is mode-
k
unidentifiable (Theorem
1
).
Taking advantage of the proposed mode-identification method, it is possible to utilize the maximally selected data section for mode-wise loading space estimation. Specifically, for each mode
k\in[K]
, we concatenate all the intervals where no change is deemed mode-
k
identifiable within, say
(\widehat{\theta}_{j_{1}},\widehat{\theta}_{j_{2}}]
for some
0\leq j_{1}<j_{2}\leq\widehat{q}+1
, and estimate the loading space therein.
Upon consistently estimating
{\mathcal{K}}_{j}
, this mode-informed approach is expected to improve upon the naive, segment-wise estimator, which we illustrate empirically in Section
5.4
.

3.3
Tuning parameter selection

Core factor dimensions.

We estimate
r_{k},\,k\in[K]
, by inspecting the eigenvalues of
\widehat{\Gamma}^{(k)}_{Y}
, say
\lambda_{\ell}(\widehat{\Gamma}^{(k)}_{Y}),\,\ell\geq 1
, in the decreasing order, for each
k\in[K]
, see (
3.1
).
Specifically, following
12
, we set
\widehat{r}_{k}=\argmax_{1\leq\ell\leq\bar{r}_{k}}\lambda_{\ell}(\widehat{\Gamma}^{(k)}_{Y})/\lambda_{\ell+1}(\widehat{\Gamma}^{(k)}_{Y})
, where
\bar{r}_{k}=\lceil p_{k}/3\rceil
is an upper bound on the factor number for mode
k
.

Weight matrix
W_{\tau}
.

The asymptotic consistency in change point detection is established for any positive definite matrix
W
with bounded spectrum, as
W_{\tau}
(see Theorem
2
).
In the context of detecting mean shifts in multivariate time series,
21
discuss the computational, numerical and statistical trade-off between the use of a weight matrix estimating the full (long-run) covariance of the noise, and that only contains its diagonal elements.
They show that none of the two choices uniformly outperforms the other in terms of the detection power, and the latter choice is to be preferred for its numerical stability for moderately large dimensions, particularly as the inverse of the matrix is used in the detector statistic.
From these considerations, we choose to use as
W
the matrix containing the diagonal entries of the following Bartlett kernel long-run covariance matrix estimator
\widehat{W}_{0}
, an approach also taken by
11
: With
\widehat{g}_{k,t}=\mathrm{vech}(\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{t})\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{t})^{\intercal}-\widehat{\Gamma}_{G,1,T}^{(k)})
and bandwidth
w=\lfloor T^{1/4}\rfloor
,

\displaystyle\widehat{W}_{0}=\widehat{\Gamma}(0)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{w}\left(1-\frac{\ell}{w+1}\right)\left(\widehat{\Gamma}(\ell)+(\widehat{\Gamma}(\ell))^{\intercal}\right),\text{\ where \ }\widehat{\Gamma}(\ell)=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=\ell+1}^{T}\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{g}_{1,t}\widehat{g}_{1,t-\ell}^{\intercal}\\
\vdots\\
\widehat{g}_{K,t}\widehat{g}_{K,t-\ell}^{\intercal}\end{bmatrix}.

Seeded intervals and trimming parameter.

We set
\mu_{T}
in (
3.4
) so that the minimum interval length is
0.5T/\log(T)
, i.e.
\mu_{T}=\log_{2}(4\log(T))
. Accordingly, we set
\varpi_{T}=0.25T/\log(T)
.

Threshold
\pi_{T,p}
for change point detection.

Theorem
2
below gives a rate range for
\pi_{T,p}
which, however, involves quantities that are unknown in practice.
This is a common issue in change point detection where a practical solution is through large-scale simulations, an approach which we also adopt here.
Specifically, we simulate tensor time series under the null model (
q=0
, see Appendix
D.1
).
For each realization, we record
\max_{(s,e]\in\mathbb{M}}\max_{s<\tau<e}\mathcal{T}_{s,\tau,e}
, take the empirical
0.9
-quantiles of these maxima over
100
realizations for each combination of dimensions and other data generating parameters, and regress them on the functions of
T
and
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})
.
We use the fitted model (
R^{2}_{\text{adj}}
is roughly 0.94) with
T
and
\widehat{r}_{k}
as regressors, to determine
\pi_{T,p}
; see Appendix
C.1
for the complete description.

Threshold
\zeta_{T,p}
for mode-identification.

Based on Theorem
3
below, we examine the rescaled statistic
\|\widehat{\Xi}_{j}^{(k)}\|/(T^{-1/2}+p^{-1})
, computed on simulated datasets (with
q=3
, see Appendix
D.1
)) while treating
\theta_{j}
as known, over varying configurations.
The empirical
0.99
-quantiles of the statistics for the modes
k\in[K]\setminus{\mathcal{K}}_{j}
, lie around
3.5
, which suggests the threshold
\zeta_{T,p}=3.5(T^{-1/2}+p^{-1})
; see Appendix
C.2
for details of the experiment.

4
Theory

4.1
Assumptions

We introduce the assumptions on the degree of serial and cross-sectional dependence in
\{\mathcal{X}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
, and the strength of the factors under the model (
2.2
).
We first define a class of general linear tensor time series.

Definition 3
.

A tensor time series
\{\mathcal{Y}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
with
\mathcal{Y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\ldots\times p_{K}}
, is a
(\nu,\beta)
-general linear tensor time series for some
\nu\geq 4
and
\beta\geq 0
, if and only if it holds that
\mathcal{Y}_{t}=\sum_{h\geq 0}a_{y,h}\mathcal{X}_{y,t-h}
where (i) the innovation process
\{\mathcal{X}_{y,t}\}
has independent elements with zero mean, unit variance, and uniformly bounded
\nu
-th order moments, i.e. denoting the elements of
\mathcal{X}_{y,t}
by
\mathcal{X}_{y,t,\boldsymbol{i}},\,\boldsymbol{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{K})
, there exists some constant
C_{\nu}>0
depending on
\nu
, such that
{\sup_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}}\max_{\boldsymbol{i}\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}[p_{k}]}\mathbb{E}(|\mathcal{X}_{y,t,\boldsymbol{i}}|^{\nu})\leq C_{\nu}
; (ii) the scalar coefficients
a_{y,h}
satisfy
\sum_{h\geq 0}a_{y,h}^{2}=1
and
\sum_{h\geq 0}|a_{y,h}|\leq c
for some constant
c>0
; and (iii) there exists some constant
c_{\beta}>0
depending on
\beta
, such that
\sup_{m\geq 0}(m+1)^{\beta}\sum_{s=m}^{\infty}|a_{y,s}|\leq c_{\beta}
.

Definition
3
formalizes the data generating process first used in
20
, which is convenient in modeling tensor time series with temporal dependence structure. Essentially, for each
\boldsymbol{i}\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}[p_{k}]
, the time series
\{\mathcal{Y}_{t,\boldsymbol{i}}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
follows a general linear process with weak serial correlation, bounded
\nu
-th moment, and
\beta
characterizing the tail summability.
By construction, the linearity of
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
in
\{\mathcal{X}_{y,t-h}\}_{h\geq 0}
, is preserved under various operations on
\mathcal{Y}_{t}
such as vectorization, unfolding and tensor reshaping. In the definition, (iii) is implied by (ii) e.g. when
\beta=0
.
With Definition
3
, and
\Delta_{j}=\min(\theta_{j}-\theta_{j-1},\theta_{j+1}-\theta_{j})
denoting the spacing between
\theta_{j}
and its neighboring change points for each
j\in[q]
, we make the following assumptions.

Assumption 1

(Core factor)
.

The series
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a
(\nu,\beta)
-general linear tensor time series.

Assumption 2

(Global factor loadings)
.

For each
k\in[K]
, there exists some constant
c_{\lambda}
such that (i) the largest elements of
\Lambda_{k}
is bounded by
c_{\lambda}
in modulus and (ii)
p_{k}^{-1}\Lambda_{k}^{\intercal}\Lambda_{k}=I_{r_{k}}
for all sufficiently large
p_{k}
.

Assumption 3

(Transformation matrices)
.

The transformation matrices satisfy the following.

(i)

For each
j\in[q+1]
and
k\in[K]
, we have
\|A_{j,k}\|_{F}
bounded away from zero and infinity.

(ii)

For each
k\in[K]
, there is
\Sigma_{A,k}
such that

T^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{q+1}\big\|\otimes_{\ell=K,\,\ell\neq k}^{1}A_{j,\ell}\big\|_{F}^{2}\cdot(\theta_{j}-\theta_{j-1})\cdot A_{j,k}A_{j,k}^{\intercal}\to\Sigma_{A,k}
as
\min(T,p_{1},\dots,p_{K})\to\infty
.

(iii)

For each
k\in[K]
,
[A_{1,k},A_{2,k},\dots,A_{q+1,k}]
has full row rank as
\min(T,p_{1},\dots,p_{K})\to\infty
.

(iv)

There exists some constant
c_{\Delta}\in(0,1/2]
such that
\min_{1\leq j\leq q}\Delta_{j}\geq c_{\Delta}T.

Assumption 4

(Idiosyncratic noise)
.

There are
(\nu,\beta)
-general linear tensor time series
\{\mathcal{F}_{e,t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{\epsilon_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
independent of each other, such that the noise
\mathcal{E}_{t}
,
t\in[T]
, admits the representation

\mathcal{E}_{t}=\mathcal{F}_{e,t}\times_{k=1}^{K}A_{e,k}+\Sigma_{\epsilon}\circ\epsilon_{t}
,
where each
A_{e,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{k}\times r_{e,k}}
satisfies
\|A_{e,k}\|_{1}=O(1)
and
\|A_{e,k}\|_{\infty}=O(1)
, and the order-
K
tensor
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
has uniformly bounded elements. Finally,
\{\mathcal{F}_{e,t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{\epsilon_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
are independent of
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

Under Assumption
1
, we have
\mathcal{F}_{t}
satisfy Condition
1
.
Assumption
2
is a tensor analogue of the condition found in
4
, and similar conditions are made in the literature on factor modeling for vector-
(
52
)
, matrix-
(
59
)
, and tensor-valued
(
12
)
time series.
Assumption
3
regularizes the transformation matrices so that, together with Assumption
2
, the global loading matrices
\Lambda_{k},\,k\in[K]
, can be estimated consistently. Part (i) prevents the factor structure from vanishing along some modes in any segment; parts (ii) and (iii), in combination with Assumption
2
, generalize the standard full column rank condition imposed on the loading matrices in factor models, to the change point setting.
In the change point literature, when
K=1
, our part (ii) is akin to Assumption 5 (iii) in
11
which constrains the covariance of the pseudo-factor rather than the transformation matrices; part (iii) boils down to Assumption 11 (i) in
25
, see also their Remark 2 for more discussion.
The linear spacing condition made in (iv) is invariably found in the related literature; see, for example,
9
;
11
;
7
;
26
.
We may relax this which, however, calls for the expansion of the interval collection
\mathbb{M}
with
\mu_{T}\asymp\log_{2}(T)
(see (
3.4
)), and has further implication on the detection and estimation performance, see Appendix
A.3
for detail.

Under Assumption
4
, the idiosyncratic component is decomposed into a factor-driven component with approximately sparse loadings, and a linear tensor time series,
and thus contains both cross-sectional and serial correlation.
At the same time, the cross-sectional correlations are sufficiently weak so that with Assumptions
2
and
3
, there exists a large gap between the
r_{k}
-th and the
(r_{k}+1)
-th largest eigenvalues of the mode-
k
covariance of
\textnormal{{mat}}_{k}(\mathcal{X}_{t})
for all
k\in[K]
, which ensures the asymptotic identifiability of the (unobserved)
\mathcal{C}_{t}
and
\mathcal{E}_{t}
across all the modes.
We specify our requirements on
(\nu,\beta)
in Assumptions
1
and
4
later; it suffices to assume that
\nu\geq 4
and
\beta\geq 0
for consistent estimation of
\Lambda_{k}
(Proposition
1
), whereas we require that
\nu>8
and
\beta>1
for the consistency in change point detection and mode-identification (Theorems
2
and
3
).

Assumption 5

(Signal-to-noise ratio)
.

The jump magnitudes
\omega_{j},\,j\in[q]
, defined in Definition
1
satisfy

\min_{j\in[q]}\,\sqrt{\Delta_{j}}\,\omega_{j}/\sqrt{\log(T)}\to\infty
and
p\min_{j\in[q]}\,\omega_{j}\to\infty
as
\min(T,p)\to\infty
.

Assumption 6

(Dimensionality)
.

p^{-1}\sqrt{T}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\log(T)})
as
\min(T,p_{1},\ldots,p_{K})\to\infty
.

Assumption
5
permits
\omega_{j}\to 0
as
\min(T,p_{1},\ldots,p_{K})\to\infty
, at a sufficiently slow rate and accordingly, the rate of estimation for each
\theta_{j}
explicitly depends on
\omega_{j}
(Theorem
2
).
Assumption
6
is weaker than the conditions on the relative divergence of
p
and
T
found in
7
;
25
;
3
;
11
for change point detection and testing, that
p^{-1}\sqrt{T}=o(1)
. Under Assumptions
3
(iv) and
6
, the second requirement in Assumption
5
implies the first.

Finally, we impose the following condition on
\Xi_{j}^{(k)}
defined in (
3.5
) for the change at
\theta_{j}
to be identifiable with the modes
k\in{\mathcal{K}}_{j}
(where
\|\Xi_{j}^{(k)}\|\neq 0
), which still allows for
\min_{k\in{\mathcal{K}}_{j}}\!\!\|\Xi_{j}^{(k)}\|\to 0
.

Assumption 7

(The degree of mode-identifiability)
.

\max(T^{-1/2},p^{-1})=o\big(\min_{j\in[q],\,k\in{\mathcal{K}}_{j}}\|\Xi_{j}^{(k)}\|\big)
.

4.2
Asymptotic results

We first establish that the column space of each loading matrix
\Lambda_{k}
in (
2.2
) is consistently estimated by that of
\widehat{\Lambda}_{k}
given in Section
3.1
.
This plays a key role in the subsequent analysis, and is of independent interest as a first-of-its-kind result derived in the presence of multiple change points under a weak moment condition.

Proposition 1

(Loading estimation)
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
hold, with
\nu\geq 4
and
\beta\geq 0
for Assumptions
1
and
4
.
Then there exists a matrix
\widehat{H}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{k}\times r_{k}}
which satisfies
\widehat{H}_{k}\widehat{H}_{k}^{\intercal}=I_{r_{k}}+o_{P}(1)
as
\min\{T,p_{1},\dots,p_{K}\}\to\infty
, such that

\frac{1}{p_{k}}\Big\|\widehat{\Lambda}_{k}-\Lambda_{k}\widehat{H}_{k}\Big\|_{F}^{2}=\mathcal{O}_{P}\left\{\frac{1}{Tp_{\text{-}k}}+\frac{1}{p^{2}}+\Big(\sum_{\ell\in[K]\setminus\{k\}}\frac{1}{Tp_{\text{-}\ell}}\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{T}+\frac{1}{p_{k}}\Big)\right\}.

Theorem 2

(Consistency of change point detection)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
–
6
hold, with
\nu>8
and
\beta>1
for Assumptions
1
and
4
. Suppose that the threshold
\pi_{T,p}
is chosen to satisfy
\sqrt{\log(T)}/\pi_{T,p}=o(1)
and
\pi_{T,p}=o(\min_{j\in[q]}\sqrt{\Delta_{j}}\omega_{j})
,

\mathbb{M}
defined in (
3.4
) is constructed with
\mu_{T}\asymp\log_{2}\log(T)
, the trimming parameter satisfies
\varpi_{T}=c_{\varpi}T/2^{\mu_{T}}
for some constant
c_{\varpi}\in(0,1/2)
, and
W_{\tau}=W
is used in (
3.3
) where
W
is a positive definite matrix with
\max(\|W\|,\|W^{-1}\|)\leq C_{W}
for some constant
C_{W}>0
. Then TFMseg (Algorithm
1
) returns a set of change point estimators
\widehat{\Theta}=\{\widehat{\theta}_{j},\,j\in[\widehat{q}]:\,\widehat{\theta}_{1}<\ldots<\widehat{\theta}_{\widehat{q}}\}
which satisfies, for any sequence
\upsilon_{T}\to\infty
arbitrarily slowly,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\widehat{q}=q,\,\max_{j\in[q]}\,\omega_{j}^{2}|\widehat{\theta}_{j}-\theta_{j}|\leq\upsilon_{T}\right\}\to 1\text{ \ as \ }\min(T,p_{1},\ldots,p_{K})\to\infty.

Theorem
2
establishes that TFMseg consistently estimates both the total number and locations of the change points, which holds for any positive definite matrix
W
with bounded spectra in place of
W_{\tau}
in (
3.3
);
the specific form of
W
may only affect the finite-sample power. This viewpoint is in line with the multivariate change point detection framework of
38
, where the weighting matrix in their CUSUM statistic is treated as a tuning parameter, and structured choices are discussed as a way of balancing between numerical stability and detection power.
The choice of
\pi_{T,p}
is compatible with Assumption
5
.
The rate of estimation for each
\theta_{j}
is characterized by the change size
\omega_{j}
, in the sense that
|\widehat{\theta}_{j}-\theta_{j}|=\mathcal{O}_{P}(\omega_{j}^{-2}\upsilon_{T})
, i.e. larger changes are estimated with better accuracy.
With
\upsilon_{T}\to\infty
arbitrarily slowly, under Assumption
5
, we have
|\widehat{\theta}_{j}-\theta_{j}|/\Delta_{j}=o_{P}(1)
, i.e. the change point estimators are consistent in the re-scaled time.
We remark that Theorem
2
is established under a condition on the tail behavior of
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\{\mathcal{E}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
characterized through
\nu>8
, which is comparable to those found in vector
(
25
;
3
)
and matrix
(
48
;
49
)
settings.
We may relax this and require
\nu>4
, which calls for e.g. a stronger condition on the signal-to-noise, namely
T^{-\nu/8+1}=o(\min_{j\in[q]}\omega_{j}\sqrt{\Delta_{j}})
, than that found in Assumption
5
.

Finally, we establish the consistency in mode-identification at each change point, a first result in its kind, which builds upon the consistency in change point detection in Theorem
2
.

Theorem 3

(Consistency of mode-identification)
.

Let all the assumptions in Theorem
2
hold, and additionally impose Assumption
7
.
Setting the mode-identification threshold
\zeta_{T,p}
to fulfill
T^{-1/2}+p^{-1}=o(\zeta_{T,p})
and
\zeta_{T,p}/\min_{j\in[q],\,k\in{\mathcal{K}}_{j}}\|\Xi_{j}^{(k)}\|=o(1)
as
\min(T,p_{1},\ldots,p_{K})\to\infty
,
we have
\mathbb{P}\big\{\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}_{j}={\mathcal{K}}_{j}\text{ for all }j\in[q]\big\}\to 1
.

5
Numerical experiments

5.1
Settings

We generate
\{\mathcal{X}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
from an order-
3
tensor factor model under (
2.1
)
with
q=3
and
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})=(3,3,3)
.
The factor process is generated as

\mathrm{vec}(\mathcal{F}_{t})=\rho_{f}\,\mathrm{vec}(\mathcal{F}_{t-1})+\varepsilon_{t}
with
\varepsilon_{t}\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}\mathcal{N}_{r}({0},\,(1-\rho_{f}^{2})I_{r})
,
and the idiosyncratic tensor
\mathcal{E}_{t}
has i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries.
The loading matrix
\Lambda_{1,k}
has its entries generated independently from the uniform distribution
\mathcal{U}(-1,1)
.
Then at each
j\in[3]
, we set
\Lambda_{j+1,k}=\Lambda_{j,k}A_{j,k}
for
k=j
while
\Lambda_{j+1,k}=\Lambda_{j,k},\,k\neq j
, where

A_{1,1}=\begin{bmatrix}0.5&0&0\\
a_{1,21}&1&0\\
a_{1,31}&a_{1,32}&1.5\end{bmatrix},\quad A_{2,2}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix},\text{ \ and \ }A_{3,3}=\begin{bmatrix}a_{3,11}&a_{3,12}&a_{3,13}\\
a_{3,21}&a_{3,22}&a_{3,23}\\
a_{3,31}&a_{3,32}&a_{3,33}\end{bmatrix},

with
a_{1,\ell h}\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
for
(\ell,h)\in\{(2,1),(3,1),(3,2)\}
, and
a_{3,\ell h}\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}\mathcal{N}(0,1/3)
for
\ell,h\in[3]
; this way, we have the dimensions of the pseudo-factor
\mathcal{G}_{t}
at
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})=(3,3,3)
while locally, the mode-
2
factor number from the third segment onward is two.
In the main text, we report the results on change point detection and mode-identification under the above model with varying
T\in\{400,800,1600,3200\}
and
(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in\{(10,10,10),(10,10,100),(10,20,40),(20,20,20)\}
, when the change points are
“unbalanced”
, i.e.
\Theta=\bigl\{\lfloor 0.25T\rfloor,\lfloor 0.5T\rfloor,\lfloor 0.625T\rfloor\bigr\}
, and the data are serially correlated with
\rho_{f}=0.7
, which we consider as the more challenging scenario. For each setting,
N=100
realizations are generated.

Additionally, we consider further change point scenarios
as well as the null scenarios (i.e.
\Theta=\emptyset
), and explore the performance of the proposed methods for change point detection and mode-identification in Appendices
D.2
and
D.3
, respectively; we verify the discussion in Remark
3
on improving the mode-wise loading space estimation via mode-identification, see Appendix
D.4
; finally, we report in Appendix
D.5
the performance TFMseg in the presence of missingness with the modification as discussed in Remark
2
.
Throughout, we apply TFMseg and the mode-identification procedure with the tuning parameters generated as in Section
3.3
.

5.2
Results for change point detection

For each
n\in[N]
, denote by
\widehat{q}^{(n)}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{(n)}
the number of change point estimators and their locations for the
n
-th realization.
Figure
1
displays the distributions of the change point estimators
\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{(n)},\,j\in[\widehat{q}^{(n)}]
, returned by TFMseg over
N=100
realizations, across varying dimensions and sample size.
We additionally report the accuracy of the change point estimators conditional on the successful detection of all three change points, by considering the realizations where
\widehat{q}^{(n)}=3
and
\widehat{\theta}^{(n)}_{j}\in((\theta_{j-1}+\theta_{j})/2,(\theta_{j}+\theta_{j+1})/2]
for all
j\in[3]
; the index set of those realizations is denoted by
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\subset[N]
.
For comparison, we consider the binary segmentation procedure of
3
which, referred to as
“LR”
, recursively performs likelihood-ratio tests.
As the method is proposed for vector time series, we first vectorize the tensor time series prior to its application.
In its implementation, we set the minimum segment length to be
0.5T/\log(T)
while all other tuning parameters are set as recommended by the authors.

Overall, the performance of both methods improves as
T
and
(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
grow, which is in line with Assumption
5
.
TFMseg tends to perform better than LR, particularly when
T
and/or
(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
are smaller, and the difference in performance is more striking when focusing on how frequently all three change points are detected by each method.
This is not too surprising as the vectorization step taken by LR ignores the tensor-structure of the data, see also Remark
1
.
The tensor-based approach is also beneficial computationally, and TFMseg is considerably faster than LR as the dimensions increase (see Figure
D.3
).
Additionally, the results in Appendix
D.2
indicate that TFMseg’s performance improves in the absence of serial correlations (with
\rho_{f}=0
), and its calibration suggested in Section
3.3
works well so that TFMseg returns few spurious estimator in the absence of any change point (see Appendix
D.2.1
).

Figure 1:
Barplots of the scaled change point estimators
\{\widehat{\theta}^{(n)}_{j}/T,\,j\in[\widehat{q}^{(n)}]\}
, returned by TFMseg and LR with varying
T\in\{400,1600\}
and
(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
(top to bottom) over
N=100
realizations. The red bars give the total frequency of estimated change points for all
n\in[N]
, while the blue bars give the frequency from the subset of realizations
n\in\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
where all three change points are detected (for each method).

5.3
Results for mode-identification

While performing the mode-identification with
\widehat{\theta}^{\pm}_{j}
defined as in (
3.6
) facilitates the theoretical analysis, for practical implementation, we propose to simply set
\widehat{\theta}^{\pm}_{j}=\widehat{\theta}_{j}
as it makes little difference numerically.
To separately treat the problem of change point detection and mode-identification, we investigate the performance of the mode-identification procedure on the subset of realizations
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
defined in Section
5.2
for each setting, where all three change points are detected by TFMseg.
Recalling that
{\mathcal{K}}_{j}\subset[K]
denotes the subset of modes for which the change at
\theta_{j}
is mode-identifiable according to Definition
2
, we report the following performance metrics:
With
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{j}^{(n)}=\{k\in[K]:\,\|\widehat{\Xi}_{j}^{(k),(n)}\|>\zeta_{T,p}\}
,

\text{TPR}_{j}=\frac{1}{|\widehat{\mathcal{D}}|}\sum_{n\in\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}\frac{|\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}_{j}^{(n)}\cap{\mathcal{K}}_{j}|}{\max(|{\mathcal{K}}_{j}|,\,1)}\text{ \ and \ }\text{FPR}_{j}=\frac{1}{|\widehat{\mathcal{D}}|}\sum_{n\in\widehat{\mathcal{D}}}\frac{|\widehat{\mathcal{K}}_{j}^{(n)}\setminus{\mathcal{K}}_{j}|}{\max(K-|{\mathcal{K}}_{j}|,\,1)}.

For the data generating process described in Section
5.1
, we have
{\mathcal{K}}_{j}=\{j\}
for all
j\in[3]
.

shows that the proposed mode-identification method performs well, with TPR and FPR improving as the sample size and the dimensions increase.
Additional numerical experiments not reported here, indicate that the relatively lower TPR when
T=400
, is attributed to that
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
is relatively small, and that the factor number estimator (see Section

) occasionally under-estimates
r_{k}
which leads to loss of signal—as evidenced by that TPR improves when
\widehat{r}_{k}
is replaced with
r_{k}
on such occasions.

reports the results from additional scenarios with more complex change structures including mode-

5.4
Results for mode-wise loading space estimation

We examine the impact of mode-identification for the downstream task of estimating the loading spaces.
To this end, we compare the
“mode-informed”
approach discussed in Remark
3
, against the naive approach that estimates the column space
\mathrm{col}(\Lambda_{j,k})
over each estimated segment for each
k\in[K]
, focusing on the single change point scenario (
q=1
) where
K=3
,
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})=(3,3,3)
,
\theta_{1}=\lfloor T/2\rfloor
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}=\{1\}
; we refer to Appendix
D.4
for the complete description of the experiments.
After applying TFMseg and the mode-identification procedure to
N=100
realizations per setting, we denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\subset[N]
, the index set of realizations where the single change is detected and
\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}_{1}
is returned.
On those realizations, noting that
\mathrm{col}(\Lambda_{1,k})=\mathrm{col}(\Lambda_{2,k})=\mathrm{col}(\Lambda_{k})
for
k\in\{2,3\}
, the mode-informed approach estimates such
\mathrm{col}(\Lambda_{k})
using the whole sample, while
\mathrm{col}(\Lambda_{j,1})
is estimated separately on
(\widehat{\theta}_{j-1},\widehat{\theta}_{j}]
for each
j\in[2]
.
We report the loading space estimation error as measured by (
D.1
) in Appendix
D.4
, see Figure
2
for a representative example.
Overall, the mode-
k
loading estimation error decreases with
p_{\text{-}k}
, in line with Proposition
1
. Also, it is apparent that the mode-informed approach benefits from pooling the information across the entire data for the modes
k\notin{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
.

Figure 2:
Boxplots of mode-wise loading estimation errors with
T=400
and varying
(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
(left to right). Segment pre (resp. Segment post) refers to segment-wise estimation errors using the data before (resp. after) the change point estimator
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
.

6
Real data applications

6.1
New York City Yellow Taxi data

We analyze the New York City (NYC) Yellow Taxi trip records released by the city’s Taxi and Limousine Commission (TLC).
1
1

1

Retrieved from
https://www.nyc.gov/site/tlc/about/tlc-trip-record-data.page
.

We focus on trips within Manhattan, which account for the majority of the observations. The pick-up and drop-off locations are aggregated into
p_{1}=p_{2}=69
pre-defined taxi zones, and each day is partitioned into
p_{3}=24
hourly intervals.
Then on each business day
t
, we observe an order-
3
tensor
\mathcal{X}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{69\times 69\times 24}
, where the entry
x_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},t}
records the number of trips from zone
i_{1}
to zone
i_{2}
during the
i_{3}
-th hourly interval, over the period from 1 January 2013 to 31 December 2022 (
T=2519
).
Inspired by the discussion on the same dataset in
19
, we set the pseudo-factor dimensions to
({r}_{1},{r}_{2},{r}_{3})=(4,2,2)
for change point detection and mode-identification; all other tuning parameters set as in Section
3.3
.
As comparison, we apply the recursive likelihood-ratio testing procedure of
3
(
“LR”
) to the vectorized time series similarly as in Section
5.2
.

TFMseg detects four change points (2013-10-16, 2014-10-15, 2016-10-19, 2020-03-06) while LR detects three (2016-03-21, 2020-03-10, 2021-04-02). The two methods agree most closely on the disruption in March 2020, with estimated break dates only a few business days apart. Outside this episode, however, the estimated change locations differ substantially, as visualized in Figure
3
.
The breaks in March 2020 has a clear interpretation: NYC confirmed its first COVID-19 case on 1 March 2020, and TLC reported that the transportation demand fell to 84% of the pre-COVID level by the beginning of April, and both TFMseg and LR detect this pandemic-related change at a plausible date.
On the other hand, the changes detected prior to 2017 may be related to the market shrinkage of traditional yellow taxi service, due to the rapidly growing app-based for-hire services. For example, TLC proceedings in October 2014 indicated that the expansion of Uber had become major regulatory concerns.
The mode-identification results shown in Figure
4
are consistent with these findings. The first two change point estimators returned by TFMseg are not assigned to any mode, reflecting a change in the overall market size.
The estimator on 2016-10-19 is associated with the pick-up and drop-off locations and appear location-related.
Interestingly, the 2020-03-06 change is associated with all three modes, reflecting again the overall change to NYC traffic pattern due to COVID-19.

Figure 3:
Daily total NYC Yellow Taxi trip volume with the change point estimators returned by TFMseg (dashed) and LR (dotted).

Figure 4:
Heatmap of the scaled mode-identification statistic
\|\widehat{\Xi}_{j}^{(k)}\|/(T^{-1/2}+p^{-1})
for the NYC taxi dataset. Rows correspond to the segments returned by TFMseg, and columns to the modes. Darker shades indicate larger scaled statistics, and highlighted borders indicate
\|\widehat{\Xi}_{j}^{(k)}\|>\zeta_{T,p}
.

6.2
Fama–French portfolio returns

We study the Fama--French portfolio returns data,
2
2

2

Retrieved from
https://mba.tuck.dartmouth.edu/pages/faculty/ken.french/Data_Library/det_100_port_sz.html
.

where the stocks are sorted into ten groups by market equity (ME) and ten groups by book-to-equity ratio (BE) to form
10\times 10
portfolios at each time stamp. We consider the time series of order-2 tensors
\mathcal{X}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{10\times 10}
on value- and equal-weighted returns, respectively, where the former weights stocks by market capitalization and the latter assigns equal weights within each portfolio.
We use the monthly data from January 1974 to June 2021 (
T=570
).
The data are not fully observed with 0.28% missingness, and complete observations are available from July 2009 only.
We leverage the approach described in Remark
2
and estimate the pseudo-factors directly from the incomplete data with
(\widehat{r}_{1},\widehat{r}_{2})=(2,2)

(
16
)
.
With all the tuning parameters set as in Section
3.3
,
TFMseg detects a single change occurring at 1998-11-01 in the value-weighted series and at 2002-06-01 in the equal-weighted series. These dates lie close to the peak of the dot-com boom; Appendix
E
presents figures that illustrate the overall market-wide variation along with the estimated change point.
When applied to the vectorized time series, LR
(
3
)
does not return any change, even when the tuning parameter controlling the minimum length of the segment, or the factor numbers are varied.

The mode-identification method does not assign this change to either mode for both series, as all scaled mode-identification statistics,
\|\widehat{\Xi}^{(k)}_{1}\|/(T^{-1/2}+p^{-1})
, fall below
3.5
(cf.
\zeta_{T,p}=3.5(T^{-1/2}+p^{-1})
).
For the equal-weighted series, however, the scaled statistics are closer to the benchmark value
3.5
, with values
2.53
for ME mode and
3.09
for BE mode, whereas the corresponding values for the value-weighted series are markedly smaller at
0.95
and
1.77
each. This suggests that, although the detected changes for both series are mode-unidentifiable, the change in the equal-weighted series exhibits a relatively stronger alignment with the portfolio categorization ME and BE. The association in the value-weighted series is weaker, possibly because value weighting downplays the heterogeneity arising from smaller firms.

7
Conclusion

This paper studies the modeling of high-dimensional tensor time series under a factor model with multiple change points.
Beyond the detection of change points, by formalizing the concept of mode-identifiable changes, we develop a statistic that consistently assigns the detected change points to their corresponding modes.
This provides an interpretation of structural changes in tensor factor models that is unavailable from detection alone,
and underpins the necessity of preserving the multi-dimensional array structure of the data.
Moreover, loading matrices for the modes where, either no change occurs or the change is not mode-identifiable, can be estimated by pooling the information across the segments, which leads to a mode-informed estimation strategy with evidently improved estimation accuracy.
We construct rigorous theoretical guarantees for our proposed methods, which is further backed up by extensive numerical results.
We remark that estimating the number of pseudo-factors,
\{r_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
, might suffer from potentially weak factors or short intervals in practice. To the best of our knowledge, a consistent estimator of factor numbers that can adapt non-linearly spaced changes remains absent in the literature, which certainly merits future investigation.

Appendix A
Additional discussions on the model and assumption

A.1
Change point scenarios and their representations

To demonstrate the generality of our model formulation in (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), we showcase some examples of different types of changes, complementing Example
1
. For this purpose, it suffices to consider a vector factor model with a single change point, as the conclusions are easily generalized to tensor factor models with multiple change points in different combinations of the modes. Below, all
\mathcal{X}_{t}
and
\mathcal{E}_{t}
are in
\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}}
, where the use of calligraphic letters are simply for consistency with the notations in (
2.1
) and (
2.2
).

First, rotational changes (i.e., a scale or space change according to Proposition
A.1
in Appendix
A.2
)
are straightforwardly encoded in our formulation. That is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{F}_{t}\times_{1}\Lambda_{1,1}+\mathcal{E}_{t}&\text{for $1\leq t\leq\theta_{1}$,}\\
\mathcal{F}_{t}\times_{1}(\Lambda_{1,1}A_{2,1})+\mathcal{E}_{t}&\text{for $\theta_{1}+1\leq t\leq T$,}\end{array}\right.

where
\Lambda_{1,1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times r_{1}}
and
A_{2,1}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times r_{1}}
are some matrices with rank
r_{1}
. In this case, the global loading matrix is
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{1,1}
, with the pre- and post-change transformation matrices
A_{1,1}=I_{r_{1}}
and
A_{2,1}
, respectively.

All other types of changes are characterized with a rank-deficient transformation matrix either before or after the change point. These include, for example, an increase in the number of factors such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{F}_{t,1}\times_{1}Z_{1}+\mathcal{E}_{t}&\text{for $1\leq t\leq\theta_{1}$,}\\
\mathcal{F}_{t,1}\times_{1}Z_{1}+\mathcal{F}_{t,2}\times_{1}Z_{2}+\mathcal{E}_{t}&\text{for $\theta_{1}+1\leq t\leq T$,}\end{array}\right.

where
Z_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times r_{1}}
and
Z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times(r_{2}-r_{1})}
, which we re-formulate as

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathcal{F}_{t,1}^{\intercal},\mathcal{F}_{t,2}^{\intercal})^{\intercal}\times_{1}(Z_{1},0_{p_{1}\times(r_{2}-r_{1})})+\mathcal{E}_{t}&\text{for $1\leq t\leq\theta_{1}$,}\\
(\mathcal{F}_{t,1}^{\intercal},\mathcal{F}_{t,2}^{\intercal})^{\intercal}\times_{1}(Z_{1},Z_{2})+\mathcal{E}_{t}&\text{for $\theta_{1}+1\leq t\leq T$,}\end{array}\right.

i.e.
\Lambda_{1}=(Z_{1},Z_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times r_{2}}
with transformation matrix
A_{1,1}
being a diagonal matrix with 1 on the first
r_{1}
elements and zeros otherwise, and
A_{2,1}=I_{r_{2}}
. A decrease in the number of factors can be formulated similarly.

Next, we consider a change in the column space of the loading matrix:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{F}_{t}\times_{1}Z_{1}+\mathcal{E}_{t}&\text{for $1\leq t\leq\theta_{1}$,}\\
\mathcal{F}_{t}\times_{1}Z_{2}+\mathcal{E}_{t}&\text{for $\theta_{1}+1\leq t\leq T$,}\end{array}\right.

where
Z_{1},Z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times u}
such that
\text{col}(Z_{1})\cap\text{col}(Z_{2})=\emptyset
. This leads to an interesting re-formulation as

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\intercal},\mathcal{G}_{t}^{\intercal})^{\intercal}\times_{1}(Z_{1},0_{p_{1}\times u})+\mathcal{E}_{t}&\text{for $1\leq t\leq\theta_{1}$,}\\
(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\intercal},\mathcal{G}_{t}^{\intercal})^{\intercal}\times_{1}(Z_{2},0_{p_{1}\times u})+\mathcal{E}_{t}&\text{for $\theta_{1}+1\leq t\leq T$,}\end{array}\right.

for some hypothetical
\mathcal{G}_{t}
as an independent copy of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
so that the core factor requirement Assumption
1
can be satisfied. The core factor rank is therefore
r_{1}=2u
according to our formulation in (
2.1
). This introduction of
\mathcal{G}_{t}
is needed to have the global loading
\Lambda_{1}=(Z_{1},Z_{2})
, which it is not possible for
r_{1}<2u
due to the different column spaces
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
span. The transformation matrices are then read as

A_{1,1}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{u}&0_{u\times u}\\
0_{u\times u}&0_{u\times u}\end{bmatrix},\quad A_{2,1}=\begin{bmatrix}0_{u\times u}&0_{u\times u}\\
I_{u}&0_{u\times u}\end{bmatrix}.

Note that
A_{1,1}
and
A_{2,1}
easily satisfy Assumption
3
(iii).

To conclude all the previous discussion, the formulation (
2.1
) or, equivalently, that in (
2.2
) is general enough to cover most, if not all (depending on the scope of interests), types of changes in the factor structure. One might argue that changes in the serial correlation in the core factors cannot be formulated, but the serial correlation is not manifested in the static factor representation anyway. This implies the merit of investigating structural changes in dynamic factor models (cf.
23
,
23
, for the vector time series case), which we leave for the future study.

A.2
An anatomy of mode-identifiable changes

We complement Theorem
1
by categorizing mode-identifiable changes into three types.

Proposition A.1

(Three types of mode-identifiable change)
.

Consider any change from
A_{j,k}
to
A_{j+1,k}
for
j\in[q]
and
k\in[K]
, and denote by
W_{j,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{k}\times r_{k}}
the diagonal matrix of singular values of
A_{j,k}
in descending order.
Let us define the types of changes as follows.

(i)

Rank change:
A_{j,k}
and
A_{j+1,k}
have different numbers of non-zero singular values.

(ii)

Scale change: The change is not a rank change, there does not exist a constant
c
such that
W_{j+1,k}=cW_{j,k}
,
and the space spanned by the non-zero singular values of
A_{j,k}
is the same as that of
A_{j+1,k}
.

(iii)

Space change: The change is not a rank change nor a scale change, and for any
i\in[r_{k}]
, the left singular space of
A_{j,k}
corresponding to
(W_{j,k})_{ii}
is different from that of
A_{j+1,k}
corresponding to
(W_{j+1,k})_{ii}
.

Then the change is mode-identifiable if and only if it falls into one of the three types above.

Proposition
A.1
provides a taxonomy of structural changes in tensor factor models, which is comparable with
that given by
26
in the context of vector time series factor modeling.
We remark that the distinctions between scale and space changes are ad-hoc, and they only serve to provide more insight on the possible changes in a tensor factor model.
It is technically difficult
to identify a scale change or a space change, due to the possibility of repeated singular values in the transformation matrix, which cannot be ruled out.
On the other hand, estimating the factor numbers
(
42
;
31
;
12
, e.g.)

can lead to the identification of rank changes.

Proof of Proposition
A.1
.

It is straightforward that when the change falls into any of the three types described in Proposition
A.1
, it is mode-identifiable according to Theorem
1
. It suffices to show if a change is mode-identifiable, then it must fall into one type (note that the types are mutually exclusive by how they are defined), or equivalently, if a change does not fall into any type, then it is mode-unidentifiable.

For any
j=1,\dots,q+1
, we denote the singular value decomposition as

A_{j,k}=Q_{j,k}W_{j,k}R_{j,k}^{\intercal},

(A.1)

for some orthogonal matrices
Q_{j,k},R_{j,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{k}\times r_{k}}
and diagonal matrix
W_{j,k}
of the singular values of
A_{j,k}
in descending order, such that
A_{j,k}A_{j,k}^{\intercal}=Q_{j,k}W_{j,k}^{2}Q_{j,k}^{\intercal}
. Then for a change from
A_{j,k}
to
A_{j+1,k}
not falling into any type, we may write

A_{j,k}=(Q_{j,k}^{\dagger}W_{j,k}^{\dagger},0_{r_{k}\times w})R_{j,k}^{\intercal},\quad A_{j+1,k}=(Q_{j+1,k}^{\dagger}W_{j+1,k}^{\dagger},0_{r_{k}\times w})R_{j+1,k}^{\intercal},

where
0_{r_{k}\times w}
is a zero matrix of dimension
r_{k}\times w
with
0\leq w<r_{k}
,
W_{j,k}^{\dagger}
represents the diagonal sub-matrix consisting of only non-zero entries in
W_{j,k}
,
Q_{j,k}^{\dagger}
represents the sub-matrix of
Q_{j,k}
with the columns corresponding the non-zero diagonal entries of
W_{j,k}
, and analogously for
W_{j+1,k}^{\dagger}
and
Q_{j+1,k}^{\dagger}
. Since the change is not of any type, we have

\mathrm{col}(Q_{j,k,i}^{\dagger})=\mathrm{col}(Q_{j+1,k,i}^{\dagger})\text{ \ for all \ }1\leq i\leq r_{k}-w,\text{ \ and \ }W_{j+1,k}^{\dagger}=cW_{j,k}^{\dagger},

where
Q_{j,k,i}^{\dagger}
denotes the column(s) of
Q_{j,k}^{\dagger}
corresponding to the
i
-th largest element in
W_{j,k}^{\dagger}
.
This implies there exists some block diagonal matrix
Q
, where each block is an orthogonal matrix corresponding to a repeated singular value, or
\pm 1
for a unique singular value, such that
Q_{j+1,k}^{\dagger}=Q_{j,k}^{\dagger}Q
. Thus,

\displaystyle\;\quad A_{j+1,k}A_{j+1,k}^{\intercal}=(Q_{j+1,k}^{\dagger}W_{j+1,k}^{\dagger},0_{r_{k}\times w})\begin{bmatrix}(W_{j+1,k}^{\dagger})^{\intercal}(Q_{j+1,k}^{\dagger})^{\intercal}\\
0_{r_{k}\times w}\end{bmatrix}=Q_{j+1,k}^{\dagger}(W_{j+1,k}^{\dagger})^{2}(Q_{j+1,k}^{\dagger})^{\intercal}

\displaystyle=c^{2}\cdot Q_{j,k}^{\dagger}Q(W_{j,k}^{\dagger})^{2}Q^{\intercal}(Q_{j,k}^{\dagger})^{\intercal}=c^{2}\cdot Q_{j,k}^{\dagger}(W_{j,k}^{\dagger})^{2}(Q_{j,k}^{\dagger})^{\intercal}=c^{2}\cdot A_{j,k}A_{j,k}^{\intercal},

where the second last equality used the fact that either the orthogonal blocks in
Q
correspond to a scale multiple of identity matrices in the diagonal matrix
(W_{j,k}^{\dagger})^{2}
, or the blocks in
Q
are diagonal with
\pm 1
corresponding to unique diagonal entries in
(W_{j,k}^{\dagger})^{2}
.

Applying Lemma
B.1
to the above, and with Theorem
1
, the change is mode-unidentifiable, as desired. This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

A.3
Relaxation of Assumption
3
(iv)

Assumption
3
(iv) requires the spacing of the change points to be linear in
T
.
We discuss how this assumption may be relaxed, by presenting an alternative assumption in Appendix
A.3.1
, and discussing an alteration to TFMseg and its impact on the theoretical results in Appendix
A.3.2
.

A.3.1
Alternative of Assumption
3

In place of Assumption
3
, let us consider the following assumption.

Assumption
3
’

(Alternative of Assumption
3
)
.

The transformation matrices satisfy:

(i)

For each
j\in[q+1]
and
k\in[K]
, we have
\|A_{j,k}\|_{F}
bounded away from zero and infinity.

(ii)

For each
k\in[K]
, there is
\Sigma_{A,k}
such that

T^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{q+1}\big\|\otimes_{\ell=K,\,\ell\neq k}^{1}A_{j,\ell}\big\|_{F}^{2}\cdot(\theta_{j}-\theta_{j-1})\cdot A_{j,k}A_{j,k}^{\intercal}\to\Sigma_{A,k}
as
\min(T,p_{1},\dots,p_{K})\to\infty
.

(iii)

The matrix
\Sigma_{A,k}
is positive definite.

In fact, Proposition
1
holds under this condition replacing Assumption
3
(see Lemma
B.10
).
Lemma
B.6
(i) in Appendix
B.2
shows that parts (iii) in both Assumptions
3
and
’
‣
A.3.1
are in fact equivalent if all change points are linearly spaced,
viz.
Assumption
3
(iv).
This indicates that Assumption
3
is mildly stronger than Assumption
’
‣
A.3.1
.
Below we further discuss the scenarios where Assumption
’
‣
A.3.1
(iii) holds even though Assumption
3
(iv) does not.

In what follows, we write

\bar{\Sigma}_{A,k}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{j=1}^{q+1}\big\|A_{j,\text{-}k}\big\|_{F}^{2}\cdot(\theta_{j}-\theta_{j-1})\cdot A_{j,k}A_{j,k}^{\intercal},

where
A_{j,\text{-}k}=\otimes_{\ell=K,\,\ell\neq k}^{1}A_{j,\ell}
.
Focusing on the mode
1
, we consider the case with
q=1
and
r_{1}=2
.

(a)

Setting
A_{1,1}=I_{2}
and
A_{2,1}=2I_{2}
, we have

\bar{\Sigma}_{A,k}=\frac{1}{T}\left\{\begin{bmatrix}\theta_{1}&0\\
0&\theta_{1}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}4(T-\theta_{1})&0\\
0&4(T-\theta_{1})\end{bmatrix}\right\}=\left(4-\frac{3\theta_{1}}{T}\right)\cdot I_{2}

which always converges to some positive definite matrix, regardless of the order of
\theta_{1}
relative to
T
.

(b)

Consider the following with
a\neq 0
(otherwise there is no change):

\displaystyle A_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}\text{ \ and \ }A_{2,1}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
a&1\end{bmatrix}.

Then,

\displaystyle\bar{\Sigma}_{A,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\left\{\begin{bmatrix}\theta_{1}&0\\
0&\theta_{1}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}T-\theta_{1}&a(T-\theta_{1})\\
a(T-\theta_{1})&(a^{2}+1)(T-\theta_{1})\end{bmatrix}\right\}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\frac{a(T-\theta_{1})}{T}\\
